A remembered handshake revision makes `connect` call `initialize()` directly (no discover round trip); a remembered *modern* era does not take this shortcut and still runs the full policy, so a modern server costs one `server/discover` round trip per connect (cheap, and deliberate); the era map is process-global and unlocked, which is safe only because there is a single asyncio event loop (squawk 0203); if that fails with an `MCPError` and no 401 was seen since it began, the full policy runs in the same call. The era is forgotten on any failed connect, on a mid-use `-32022`, on source update (`update_mcp_source`, after the write succeeds), on delete and on Reset connection. **Test connection** passes `fresh_negotiation=True` (it always re-negotiates, so a server upgraded since the last Test is noticed) and `record_era=False` when the Test's body changed the server under test: `test_mcp_connection` computes `override_applied` as "effective `type` or `config` (minus `_cache`) differs from the saved row" (the editor sends its form body on every Test, so a body alone is not an override; secrets are not compared) and threads `record_era` to the no-auth helper, the stdio helper and the Phase A client. `record_era=False` suppresses both remember and forget. **A 401 on `server/discover` (leg `discover-401-signin`)** is the one departure from `negotiate_auto`, taken only when the sign-in record (`_signin_record`) shows a 401 since that attempt's own baseline (checked before the -32022 branch, whatever the error code): `negotiate` still tries `initialize()` (a server with discover gated behind sign-in and `initialize` public keeps connecting on the handshake), but if that raises an `MCPError` it raises `DiscoverSignInRequired(resource_metadata_url)` (captured when the 401 was seen, because the failed handshake overwrites the record's last status) and `_classify_failure` row 2 maps it to `MCPAuthRequired`. With no record (stdio, mock sessions) the loop is the SDK's. Trade-offs: the first connect after each process start pays one discover round trip against a handshake-only server (a handshake-only server that answers discover with an error costs milliseconds; one that silently ignores discover costs up to the 10 s discover timeout, once per run per source; later connects skip it); a connect from a pooled client never `fresh_negotiation`s. **A modern connection has no session id**, so a mid-use 404 reads "Not Found" and never self-heals (Flight 1's DD12.3 now covers every modern-era connection). A modern `server/discover` from the `mcp` 2.3.0 SDK's own servers stamps `serverInfo` with `version: ''` and no `_meta` (real modern servers report real versions: sonos 0.6.1, docs-ai-search 0.4.13 and cloudflare-api 0.1.0 in the Flight 2 HAT); the stamp key is dropped from the persisted `_meta`. The 1 MiB SSE event cap applies only to SSE responses, which in practice means the handshake era (a modern single-response POST is plain JSON); the cap tests pin the handshake era through `_era.remember_era`.
   - **Auth-posture probe, four verdicts (Mission 29 F2 DD9).** `_probe_auth_posture` (an `httpx2` POST of a bare `initialize`) returns `"no_auth"` (200), `"oauth"` (401), **`"unexpected_status"`** (any other HTTP status: the server answered but the probe cannot classify it, e.g. a 2026-07-28-only server answering `initialize` with 400) or `"error"` (transport error, timeout, any other exception; fails fast with `connection probe failed`, never tries the SDK). On `"unexpected_status"`, `test_mcp_connection` Phase 0 runs the no-auth SDK path (`_build_and_test_no_auth_mcp(..., from_fallthrough=True)`), which negotiates, so such a server connects. A success persists `auth_posture: "no_auth"` as that helper always does, so the next Test skips the probe (intended). If the SDK path hits a 401 the helper returns the module sentinel `_SIGNIN_REQUIRED` instead of the race-branch error; the route then calls `_persist_oauth_posture` (the helper Phase 0's `"oauth"` branch also uses) and continues into Phase B. When the probe itself said `"no_auth"`, the race branch is unchanged (an error envelope). **Sortie 04 (rejected keys):** in that helper a `MCPCredentialsRejected` (operator headers + a bare 401 with no `resource_metadata`) returns `_SIGNIN_REQUIRED` under `from_fallthrough` exactly like `MCPAuthRequired` and otherwise falls to the typed arm's error envelope (path (a)); Phase B's path (c) is described under "MCP error translation".
   - **OAuth flow drivers send the handshake header (Mission 29 F2 DD10).** `_drive_flow_test` (Test connection Phase B) and `_drive_flow` (`POST /api/oauth/start`) keep their `initialize` body, add `MCP-Protocol-Version: <the_one.mcp._HANDSHAKE_OFFER_VERSION>` and take `params.protocolVersion` from the same constant, both read at call time. The header makes the SDK's `OAuthClientProvider` include the RFC 8707 `resource` parameter (it does so for a version of 2025-06-18 or later), matching the SDK path whose discover carries 2026-07-28. **Amendment:** when Phase B is entered from DD9's fall-through (the server already rejected `initialize`), `_drive_flow_test` instead posts `server/discover` with `MCP-Protocol-Version: LATEST_MODERN_VERSION` (read at call time); that sign-in therefore also includes `resource`. Known remaining difference: on the handshake shortcut the SDK sends `initialize` with no version header, so a 401 there omits `resource`. Tests: `tests/test_mcp_test_connection_negotiation.py`, `tests/test_oauth_start_endpoint.py`, `tests/test_auth_posture_probe.py`. Patch-target note: a test that stubs the driver's client alongside a real `httpx2` client must replace `api.httpx2` with a proxy (see that test file), because patching `httpx2.AsyncClient` changes the class identity the transport's `isinstance` check relies on.

For runtime invariants (test-seam pattern, AsyncExitStack composition, `_DEFAULT` sentinel rationale, env-var assembly, sandbox-wrap composition, pool semantics), see `the_one/mcp/README.md`.

### Sandbox seam — platform-wide (M14 F1+F2, authoritative for F4+)

**Scope (M14 F1+F2)**: the sandbox is now a platform-wide seam, not an MCP-only one. Four
spawn paths route through a single `get_sandbox_backend()` factory: built-in `shell` tool,
built-in `python_exec` tool, stdio MCP servers, and PTY `shell_session_*` sessions (wired
in M14 F2).

**Backend selection (`get_sandbox_backend()` in `the_one/platform/sandbox/factory.py`)**:
returns a `BubblewrapSandbox` on Linux when `bwrap` is in `$PATH`, and a
`PassthroughSandbox` otherwise (off-Linux or bwrap missing on Linux). On Linux with a
missing `bwrap`, a one-time WARNING is emitted; no `SandboxError` is raised at construction
time. The factory is the sole selector — spawn sites do NOT construct backends directly.

**`PassthroughSandbox`**: a no-op backend that returns the command/args/env unchanged.
Proves the seam contract without enforcing containment. Used for non-Linux hosts, for
bwrap-absent Linux environments (with logged warning), and for all passthrough-pinned
unit tests (`test_execution_tools.py`). The passthrough is observable (logged) — absence
of enforcement is not silent.

**Built-in-tool profile (host-workspace, M14 F1 L4)**:
`_build_tool_profile(exec_cfg, fs_cfg)` in `the_one/tools/execution.py` derives a
`SandboxProfile` for `shell` and `python_exec`:
- `writable_paths` = `FilesystemConfig.path_allowlist` (operator-configured workspace).
- `readonly_overlay_paths` = `FilesystemConfig.path_denylist` (denylisted entries are
  bound read-only via `--ro-bind-try`; write attempts raise; **read-scoping is satisfied
  for the boxed path** (box has no user FS — canary files are absent by construction);
  **host-workspace read-scoping still deferred** — denylisted secrets are not writable,
  not necessarily unreadable on the host path).
- `home` = real `$HOME` (the host-workspace profile gives the agent a capable assistant
  posture, not a cage; `/tmp` default is for MCP servers where no real workspace applies).
- `network_allowed` = `ExecutionConfig.sandbox_network_allowed` (deny-by-default; False
  unless the operator opts in).

1. **Strict-by-default trust** — Locally-spawned stdio MCP servers run in a bubblewrap jail: read-only filesystem outside an operator-configured `writable_paths` list; network OFF unless `network_allowed=True`. Operator-trust threat model: the sandbox does not defend against operator mistakes (broad `writable_paths`, `network_allowed=True` on untrusted sources), only against MCP-author mistakes / supply-chain compromise within the sandboxed scope.

2. **Profile storage** — `MCPSource.config["sandbox"] = {"writable_paths": list[str], "network_allowed": bool}` lives plaintext alongside `command`/`args`/`env`. Both fields optional; defaults `[]` and `false`. Namespace-flag fields (`pid_unshare`, `ipc_unshare`, `uts_unshare`, bool-defaulting-True) are JSON-edit-only advanced knobs — API validator passes them through unchanged.

3. **`ToolSandbox` Protocol** — `prepare_spawn(profile, command, args, env, cwd=None) -> SpawnSpec`. `SpawnSpec` is `@dataclass(frozen=True)` with `command/args/env/cwd` plus Windows-only `creation_flags: int = 0` and `attribute_list`. Windows fields exist purely so a future backend can populate them without changing the Protocol.

4. **`SandboxError` boundary** — Failures BEFORE bwrap successfully wraps-and-execs raise `SandboxError` (writable_paths entry doesn't exist, `EPERM` on `unshare`, container lacks §10 privilege grants). Failures AFTER (target spawned but exits non-zero, init handshake fails) raise `MCPConnectionError` / `MCPVersionMismatch`. **Bwrap missing** no longer raises `SandboxError` at construction time — the factory routes to `PassthroughSandbox` (one-time WARNING on Linux; no error). The runtime bwrap-stderr→`SandboxError` translation on the MCP stdio path (catalog messages in `the_one/mcp/errors.py`) still raises `SandboxError` on post-exec bwrap failures. `THE_ONE_SANDBOX_DISABLED` is never read in production paths. Catalog messages (operator-facing, verbatim-tested) live in `the_one/mcp/errors.py`.

5. **Privilege model** — Dev container (now retired): `cap_add: [SYS_ADMIN]` AND `security_opt: [seccomp:unconfined]` (BOTH — SYS_ADMIN alone is filtered by docker's default seccomp profile). Host-native dev runs bwrap directly with the host's own privileges, so those container grants no longer apply; the seccomp/SYS_ADMIN semantics are retained here as a record of what bwrap needs when it IS containerized. Browser-test CI: no privilege grants; `THE_ONE_SANDBOX_DISABLED=1` AND `THE_ONE_TEST_MODE=1` (BOTH; set ONLY by `tests/conftest.py::pytest_configure`). Production: no flags — bwrap is mandatory.

   **Operational guardrail**: `THE_ONE_TEST_MODE` MUST NOT appear in any Dockerfile, compose env, entrypoint script, k8s manifest, or CI variable. Presence outside pytest is a deployment defect.

6. **HOME policy** — `BubblewrapSandbox.prepare_spawn` sets `env["HOME"]` from `profile.home` when set, otherwise defaults to `"/tmp"` (MCP safe default — inherited `HOME=/root` would be read-only-but-not-writable under `--ro-bind / /`; subprocess writes to `~/.cache` would EACCES; `/tmp` is writable via `--tmpfs`). The host-workspace profile (`_build_tool_profile`) passes the real `$HOME` so built-in tools get a capable-assistant posture. Operators still cannot override HOME via the kv-table — `SandboxProfile.from_dict` intentionally omits `home` from its parsed-key set.

### the_one/platform/sandbox/

Four-module package implementing the cross-platform sandbox seam (M14 F1+F2):

- **`base.py`** — defines `SandboxProfile` (policy dataclass: `writable_paths`,
  `readonly_overlay_paths`, `network_allowed`, namespace-unshare flags, `home`),
  `SpawnSpec` (frozen dataclass: `command/args/env/cwd` + Windows-only `creation_flags`
  and `attribute_list` for forward-compat), and the `ToolSandbox` `Protocol`
  (`runtime_checkable`; structurally satisfied — no inheritance required).
- **`factory.py`** — `get_sandbox_backend() -> ToolSandbox`: sole backend selector.
  Returns `BubblewrapSandbox` on Linux when `bwrap` is in `$PATH`; falls through to
  `PassthroughSandbox` on non-Linux, bwrap-absent Linux (one-time WARNING), or test
  double-flag bypass (`THE_ONE_SANDBOX_DISABLED=1` + `THE_ONE_TEST_MODE=1`). Re-evaluates
  on every call (not memoized) so test monkeypatching is always visible.
- **`bubblewrap.py`** — `BubblewrapSandbox`: wraps the target command in a bwrap PID
  namespace. `prepare_spawn(profile, command, args, env, cwd) -> SpawnSpec` emits the
  bwrap argv: `--die-with-parent`, `--unshare-user/pid/ipc/uts/net`, `--clearenv`,
  `--setenv` per env key, `--ro-bind / /`, `--tmpfs /tmp`, `--bind` per `writable_paths`,
  `--ro-bind-try` per `readonly_overlay_paths` (after writable mounts so overlays win),
  `--proc /proc`, `--dev /dev`, `[--chdir cwd]`, `--`, then the wrapped command and args.
- **`passthrough.py`** — `PassthroughSandbox`: no-op backend; returns command/args/env/cwd
  unchanged. Forward-compat shim for non-Linux hosts and test environments.

**The `prepare_spawn → SpawnSpec` contract** is the seam's public surface. All four spawn
sites call `get_sandbox_backend().prepare_spawn(profile, command, args, env, cwd)` and
consume the returned `SpawnSpec` — none construct a backend directly. The four sites are:
`ShellTool` (`tools/shell.py`), `PythonExecTool` (`tools/python_exec.py`),
`connect_stdio` in `mcp/transports/stdio.py` (MCP stdio servers), and
`UnixPtySession.start()` in `platform/pty.py` (PTY `shell_session_*` — added M14 F2).

### MCP error translation

`MCPClient.{connect, list_tools, call_tool}` raise one of: `MCPVersionMismatch`, `MCPAuthRequired`, `MCPCredentialsRejected`, `SandboxError` (stdio only), or `MCPConnectionError` (catchall). `MCPToolProvider.get_tools_for_agent` graceful-skips all of them. Adding another typed exception requires updating BOTH the translator AND the consumer's match list.

**`MCPCredentialsRejected` (sortie 04 DD4).** A subclass of `MCPConnectionError` (so every existing graceful-skip catch handles it: the agent build's Path A, `_tool.py`'s "unavailable" string, `_build_and_test_no_auth_mcp`'s typed arm), with `# noqa: N818`. Its rule lives in ONE factory, `MCPClient._signin_signal(resource_metadata_url)`, the only place `MCPAuthRequired` is built for a 401 (`_auth_required_since` and `_classify_failure`'s `DiscoverSignInRequired` row both call it; `_oauth_flow_auth_required`, used by the three `except* (OAuthFlowError, OAuthTokenError)` arms, which only run with a provider attached, is separate and unchanged). It returns `MCPCredentialsRejected` exactly when the source is `streamable-http` and `build_request_headers(self.source)` is non-empty (any operator header or non-OAuth-managed secret, per-agent overrides included, imported function-locally for the import guard), the 401 named no `resource_metadata`, and `self._auth_provider is None`; otherwise `MCPAuthRequired`. `_classify_failure` calls `_mark_dead()` when it returns a rejection and `op != "connect"`, so a corrected header takes effect on the next acquire. A rejected tool call reaches the agent loop's generic arm (`[error] Tool execution failed: ...`, no `is_auth_required` metadata), so chat offers no login button. **Test connection** (`api.py::test_mcp_connection`): (a) cached `no_auth` whose key is now rejected returns `{"status": "error", "error": "MCPCredentialsRejected: ..."}` and keeps `no_auth` (an earlier 200 proved a header-auth server); (b) on DD9's fall-through the rejection is treated like `MCPAuthRequired` (`_SIGNIN_REQUIRED`) so Phase B decides; (c) when Phase B was entered from THIS request's probe `"oauth"` verdict or the fall-through (both sent the operator headers and got 401; Phase B's own requests carry no operator headers and still must not), `_drive_flow_test` classifies a failed `/register` (`OAuthRegistrationError` starting "Registration failed:") with operator headers, an `OAuthClientProvider`, and both `provider.context.protected_resource_metadata` and `.oauth_metadata` `None` as a rejection (a private sentinel on the consent future; the route returns the same envelope and removes `_cache.auth_posture` via `_clear_auth_posture`, since no request ever succeeded). Every other failure keeps `auth_flow_error` / `consent_url_timeout`. The self-configuration loopback source (it carries `secrets["Authorization"]`) reads as rejected when it answers 401. Tests: `tests/test_mcp_signin_detection.py`, `tests/test_mcp_test_connection_negotiation.py`; fixtures `tests/fixtures/mcp/asgi_401.py` (`resource_metadata=None`) and `OAuthASStub(bare_401=True)`.

**UI presentation (M25 F6 DD19)** — the client shows a plain headline, not the raw text: `App.mcpErrorMessage(raw)` / `App.buildMcpError(raw, testid)` (in `static/js/mcp-error.js`, used by the agent Tools tab and the Library MCP editor's connection result) map the API's `"{Class}: {message}"` to a headline by class name and known phrase, and keep the raw text, unchanged, in a collapsed "Technical details" `<details>`. No backend change; the cancelled path's masked original cause is issue #226.

**The last connection check from Studio (M31 F5 L08a, DD6).** `POST /api/mcp-sources/{id}/test/connect` is now a thin wrapper over `_test_mcp_connection_inner(source_id, request) -> (result, recordable)`. When `recordable` and `result["status"]` is `ok`, `error` or `auth_required`, the wrapper calls `MCPSourceRepository.merge_cache(id, {"last_check": {"status", "at": time.time()}})` (best-effort: a failure is logged at debug and swallowed) and returns `result` unchanged. **What records:** a body-less Test of the saved server, from either Studio caller (the editor's Test and the agent Tools tab's MCP servers tools load). **What does not:** any Test with a body (the client sends one only when its form is dirty; even a body equal to the saved row is not "the saved server"), a 404, an invalid or non-object body, and the unsupported-type return (a config-shape problem, not a check). `recordable` (no body at all) and `record_era` (type or config differs) are deliberately different gates: a body that changes only secrets still records the era but never `last_check`; do not unify them. The record holds only `status` and `at` (seconds, like `last_updated`): no error text, secret or header value. `merge_cache(source_id, fields, delete=())` reads and writes `config` in one `write_transaction`, merges `fields` into `_cache` (a `None` value is stored as null, never a delete), drops the `delete` keys, and changes no other column and not `updated_at` (nothing reads it for MCP sources). Every `_cache` write goes through it: the three ok writes (the stdio and no-auth helpers and the Phase A/B ok write) and the two posture helpers. **Intended behaviour change:** the Phase A/B ok write used to replace `_cache` wholesale and so dropped `auth_posture` on every ok; under the merge an existing `oauth` posture survives (the pool reads it, `_pool.py:203`). A PUT keeps the whole `_cache` (`last_check` included) except that a PUT changing `type` or `config.url` drops `last_check` and `auth_posture` (beside `forget_era`, in `update_mcp_source`; the tools cache stays, and an emptied `_cache` is removed); Reset drops it. The OAuth token write (`oauth/storage.py`, a whole-row `repo.update` with an `await` in between) can overwrite a `merge_cache` landing in that gap; DD6 accepts this lost update. The client derives the state with `App.usage.mcpState(source)` (`{state: 'signin'|'error'|'connected'|'none', tools, checkedAt}`, `checkedAt` in seconds) and the words with `App.usage.mcpStateLabel(state)`; precedence: `signin` when no access token and (`oauth_active` or last check `auth_required`), then `error` when last check `error`, then `connected` when tools are cached, else `none` (so a stale `auth_required` with a token and cached tools reads `connected`). The editor posts a body only when its form is dirty (clean: no body and `collectFormData` is not called), and both clients invalidate `mcp-sources` on every outcome.

**Nested `try/except*` in `connect()`** — Python (PEP 654) forbids mixing `except` and `except*` in one block. The OAuth `except*` nests inside an inner `try` so the version-mismatch `except RuntimeError` can stay flat. `list_tools`/`call_tool` use a flat shape because they have no `except` siblings. Don't "tidy up" the asymmetry.

**Sign-in detection is a sequenced 401 record, not an exception (Mission 29 DD1).** `mcp` 2.x no longer surfaces HTTP 401: a POST 401 becomes `MCPError(INTERNAL_ERROR, "Server returned an error response")` and `WWW-Authenticate` is dropped (a 401 whose body is a JSON-RPC error surfaces the server's own code, so nothing may gate on the code or text). The transport therefore attaches an async, headers-only `httpx2` **response hook** to the client it hands the SDK, on all three client branches (production client, `_test_http_client_override` seam client — hook appended on entry and removed on exit, because the seam client is shared across connections — and the factory) of the one `_open_streamable_http_transport_stack` (`connect_streamable_http` is a thin `@asynccontextmanager` over it). The hook records only **MCP POSTs** (method POST, same origin as the endpoint, `accept` naming `text/event-stream`), so token/refresh/DCR POSTs, discovery GETs and the standalone GET stream are never recorded; each response bumps a per-connection `seq` and keeps `last_status` and, for a 401, the `resource_metadata` field. The record (`AuthRecord`, in the leaf module `the_one/mcp/_signin_record.py` so `MCPClient` does not import the transport and `agent.core` still does not load `the_one.mcp.oauth`) is keyed by the yielded `ClientSession` in a `WeakKeyDictionary`; read it through `auth_record_for(session)`. `MCPClient` captures `seq` when an operation starts (connect: 0) and `_auth_required_since(record, baseline)` returns `MCPAuthRequired(resource_metadata_url=…)` **only if the most recent MCP-POST response after that baseline was a 401** — so a sign-in that succeeds mid-operation then hits an ordinary JSON-RPC error does not misfire, a flow that dies after the 401 (mismatched issuer, token endpoint 400, or a 2.3.0 bare `CancelledError` instead of `OAuthFlowError`) leaves 401 last, and a stale 401 from an earlier operation sits before the baseline. Every failure after a request was sent (`MCPError` of any code, `BaseExceptionGroup`, `CancelledError`, generic `Exception`, and `asyncio.TimeoutError`) consults the record first; `connect` reads it into a local the moment the session exists, before its cleanup branches clear `self._session`. Errors raised before sending (`MCPToolNotFoundError`, `MCPArgumentError`) never consult it. The inner `except* (OAuthFlowError, OAuthTokenError)` → `MCPAuthRequired` (no `resource_metadata_url`) stays for 1.x parity. Tests: `tests/test_mcp_signin_detection.py` (real 401s over loopback / ASGI, never an injected `HTTPStatusError`).

**`MCPAuthRequired` propagation** — `connect()`'s outer `except (MCPClientError, NotImplementedError): … raise` already propagates `MCPAuthRequired` cleanly. `list_tools`/`call_tool` DO need an explicit `except MCPAuthRequired: raise` guard because their outer `except MCPError` would re-wrap. Asymmetry by design.

**The failure matrix, the owner-task rule, the stale-token clear and the input-required error (Mission 29 Flight 2 leg 1).**

- **`MCPClient._classify_failure(exc, *, op, baseline, record=None, tool_name=None)`** builds the typed exception for the shared rows; each of `connect`/`list_tools`/`call_tool` keeps its own `try`/`except`/`except*` shape and cleanup (connect's 3-second `_safe_cleanup`, abandoning the stack on `CancelledError`, reading the sign-in record before cleanup). The three `except* (OAuthFlowError, OAuthTokenError)` blocks share only `_oauth_flow_auth_required()`. First match wins: (1) `UrlElicitationRequiredError` passes through unchanged in `list_tools`/`call_tool`, but in `connect` stays wrapped as `MCPConnectionError` (nothing outside `client.py` handles it; a pass-through would crash agent builds); (2) a 401 as the last MCP-POST response since the baseline gives `MCPAuthRequired` (for `MCPError`, `BaseExceptionGroup`, timeouts, `httpx2.HTTPError`/`anyio.ClosedResourceError`, generic exceptions and an owner-task `CancelledError`); (3) any `MCPError(-32022)`: "shared version" means `HANDSHAKE_PROTOCOL_VERSIONS + MODERN_PROTOCOL_VERSIONS`; in `connect`, a `supported` list that parses and shares nothing gives `MCPVersionMismatch` (`observed` = the list joined with ", "), anything else falls through to the generic `MCPConnectionError`; in `list_tools`/`call_tool` it always marks the client dead and calls `_era.forget_era(source.id)`, then raises `MCPVersionMismatch` (nothing shared) or `MCPConnectionError` ("the server changed protocol revision; the connection will be re-established on the next call"; also when `data` is absent or does not parse); (4) the SSE cap (`MCPConnectionError` in connect and list_tools, `MCPCallError` in call_tool; never marks dead); (5) session terminated (marks dead). Row 6 is the input-required result branch below. Every other cell keeps its earlier behaviour. `MCPVersionMismatch`'s text names the range built from `mcp.types.version` ("the-one supports 2024-11-05 through 2026-07-28"); `.pinned` still holds `_HANDSHAKE_OFFER_VERSION`.
- **Token-failure strings are a contract.** `the_one/mcp/errors.py::TOKEN_FAILURE_MARKERS` (`"did not respond"`, `"cancelled during initialize"`) are the two phrases Test connection's `looks_like_token_failure` (`api.py`) matches in an `MCPConnectionError` to decide the stored tokens are stale. `client.py` builds those two messages from the constants and `api.py` imports them; `tests/test_mcp_failure_matrix.py` pins the literal strings and produces each message through the real code path.
- **Cancellation is translated only on the owner task.** `connect()` records `self._owner_task = asyncio.current_task()` before entering the transport stack (reset on every connect, cleared in `disconnect`). In `list_tools`/`call_tool`, a `CancelledError` caught on any other task re-raises (anyio delivers its internal cancel-scope fallout to the host task via `task.cancel()`, so `cancelling()` cannot tell it from a real cancel; task identity can). On the owner task the sign-in record is consulted as before. `_owner_task is None` (never connected, or after `disconnect`) counts as "not owner".
- **Test connection clears stale OAuth secrets before a fresh sign-in.** `api.py::_clear_stale_oauth_secrets` re-reads the STORED row, strips `dcr_credentials`, `access_token`, `refresh_token` and `token_expires_at`, and writes that row back (never the request-body override), then returns the in-memory `source` with the same keys stripped but its override config intact, so Phase B signs in against the URL being tested. Both the Phase A `except MCPAuthRequired` branch and the `looks_like_token_failure` strip use it. A deleted source (`KeyError`) skips the write. The agent's pool path still raises `MCPAuthRequired` without touching the stored row.
- **A tool asking for input is a plain error.** `call_tool` passes `allow_input_required=True` to `session.call_tool`, and an `mcp.types.InputRequiredResult` (checked before the content is flattened; it has no `content`) raises `MCPCallError` with `server_message` "the server asked for extra input, which the-one can't provide yet".

**Synthetic csid pattern** — Three csid shapes pass through `oauth/handlers.py::redirect_handler`: real csids (lowercase hex: 12 characters server-allocated, 32 client-allocated by the WebSocket `start_session` action), `f"test:{source_id}"` (Test connection endpoint), `f"agent_build:{source.id}"` (pool entry-worker). Disjoint by character-set construction — hex cannot produce `:`. Synthetic csids no-op the `auth_required` broadcast.

**Auth-pause state is set before the yield** (Sortie 04 HAT): `Agent._dispatch_tool_calls` sets `_deferred_tool_calls = tcs[i:]` BEFORE it yields an auth-required tool message, because `_process_and_broadcast` and `_resume_paused_session` `break` out of the generator at that message and code after the yield never runs. Setting it afterwards left the state `None`, so `oauth_callback` never scheduled a resume and the reconnect `auth_required` replay never fired. Pinned with a real `Agent` in `tests/test_agent.py::TestPauseStateSetBeforeYield` and `tests/test_app_websocket_pause_resume.py` (`test_real_agent_*`). `resume_after_auth` also removes the auth-required placeholder tool messages (matched on both the call id and the `is_auth_required` metadata) for the deferred calls before re-dispatching them, so a call never ends up with two tool results.

### Tool authorship contract (F4+)

Tools subclass `Tool` and declare:

- `Config: type[BaseModel]` — per-tool config schema (per-category Config classes: `FilesystemConfig`, `ExecutionConfig`/`PythonExecConfig`, `WebConfig`).
- `Requires: tuple[type[BaseModel], ...]` — opts into the shared `cross_configs` view.
- `__init__(config, cross_configs=None, agent_name=None)`.
- `execute(**kwargs) -> str`.
- `truncation_direction: Literal["head", "tail"]` — which end the context-budget governor preserves when it truncates this tool's result. Default `"head"` (prose/pages/files); execution tools that return shell output (`shell`, `python_exec`, `shell_session_send`) override to `"tail"` so the `[exit code: N]` suffix + stderr tail survive. See "Context-budget governor" below.

The two-clause `try/except` envelope (catch `ConstraintError` → `[error] [constraint] {e}`, catch other exceptions → `[error] {e}`) IS the authorship contract. `ConstraintError` lives at `the_one.tools.base` (canonical) for that reason.

**Context-budget governor (M16 F08 L01 / DD1).** `Agent._execute_tool` is the single chokepoint where EVERY `ToolResult.content` — from any tool — is bounded before it enters the conversation, so no one tool result can consume a disproportionate share of the model's context window (the F07 context-poison theme). The pure helper `the_one/agent/_governor.py::govern_tool_content(content, max_context_tokens, *, fraction, floor, ceiling, direction, bytes_per_token)` computes `budget = clamp(int(fraction * max_context_tokens * bytes_per_token), floor, ceiling)` and slices on a byte boundary (`decode(errors="replace")`), appending (head) / prepending (tail) a truncation marker that sits OUTSIDE the budget. It governs the normal `output` and the timeout/generic-except error strings; the MCP `<auth required>` sentinel is NOT governed. `max_context_tokens` ≤0/None/absurd floors to 128k. Defaults — fraction 1/8, floor 8 KB, ceiling 256 KB — are env-overridable (`THE_ONE_TOOL_RESULT_BUDGET_FRACTION` / `_FLOOR` / `_CEILING`; see `.env.example`). This RETIRED the per-tool LLM-facing caps (the web text cap; the model-sized role of `output_cap_bytes`); the transport/OOM safety caps (`response_size_cap_bytes` 1 MB streaming abort, `read_size_cap_bytes` file-size guard, `output_cap_bytes` as a transport ceiling) remain — a different axis (what the process buffers/reads vs. what reaches the model).

**Tool policy resolver** (`the_one/tools/resolver.py`) composes tool-level config with agent-level overrides per per-key marker semantics on the tool's `Config` schema:

- `Annotated[type, Narrow]` — agent override must not widen the value (bool, int/float lower-cap, list/tuple subset). Other types raise `NotImplementedError`.
- `Annotated[type, NarrowPrefix]` — `list[str]` of paths is narrowing iff every agent entry is descendant-of (or equal to) at least one tool entry. Used by `FilesystemConfig.path_allowlist`.
- `Annotated[type, NarrowSuperset]` — `list[T]` is narrowing iff every tool entry is also in the agent value (agent ⊇ tool — agent adds restrictions). Used by `FilesystemConfig.path_denylist`. Membership uses raw `==`; canonicalisation is consumer-side. Since sortie 07 an agent's value for a denied list is the entries it ADDS, and the effective list is the **union** of the app-wide list and the agent's (`combine_field`), so an app-wide addition reaches every agent; strict `resolve` still checks the superset form for a value that is handed in whole.
- `Annotated[type, NarrowAnyWhenEmpty]` (sortie 07) — a `list[str]` whose empty value means "any" (`WebConfig.host_allowlist`). An empty app-wide list lets an agent list stand; a non-empty one requires every agent entry to be inside it, and an agent `[]` is "any" and therefore widens a non-empty app-wide list. `NO_HOSTS = "none.invalid"` is the code-enforced "no hosts" entry: a list holding it refuses every URL (`_url_check`), and a clip that leaves nothing produces `[NO_HOSTS]`, never `[]`.
- `Annotated[type, Substitute]` — agent override replaces (no comparison).
- Unmarked field — agent override raises `ValueError("... no Narrow/Substitute marker ...")`.

Session-bound freshness: mid-session operator updates apply at the next `AgentFactory.create()`.

**Two entry points (sortie 07).** `resolve` stays strict: it raises `OverrideWidensError` (a `ValueError`) on a widening or invalid override, and is what the save path's cross-field step and the tests use. `clip_override(schema, tool_config, agent_override) -> ClipResult` is its total twin: it never raises for any JSON value and returns the override narrowed to fit plus a list of changes (kind `narrowed`, `invalid` or `coupled`). `BuiltinToolProvider.get_tools` clips every override before `resolve`, so a stale override can no longer fail a chat build. See "Agent override validation and clipping" below.

**Cross-Config dispatch**: tools declaring `Requires` receive a per-`get_tools()`-call shared `cross_configs: dict[type[BaseModel], BaseModel]` view. Configs are `frozen=True` so one tool cannot mutate the view to corrupt another's read. Both `BuiltinToolProvider` and tests inject via the same dict (e.g. fake `SearchBackend` for tests).

### Agent override validation and clipping (sortie 07)

An agent's `tool_overrides` can only narrow the app-wide tool config (the markers above). Sortie 07 made that rule checkable at save time, survivable at build time and visible afterwards.

- **Save refuses what it can name.** `create_agent` and `update_agent` call `override_validation.validate_overrides(stored, incoming, rows)` (blocking, so in `asyncio.to_thread`; one `ToolConfigRepository.list()` per request) after the credential-override guard and before the provider check. Only a **new or changed** field is checked, against the app-wide row merged over schema defaults; an unchanged stored entry is never re-checked, so a stale value cannot block saving something else. A failure is a 422 list of `{"type": "tool_override", "loc": ["tool_overrides", tool, field], "msg": sentence}` (an unknown tool has `loc ["tool_overrides", tool]`; a non-dict entry keeps the plain `AgentConfig` 422). The field-level checks are `resolver.check_field` and the cross-field step (`default <= max`) over the changed fields plus the unchanged ones passed through `clip_override`.
- **Build clips, never fails.** `get_tools` runs `clip_override` first (total: strings for ints, floats, non-list paths, `~nosuchuser`, symlink loops, NUL bytes are all handled), then strict `resolve`. A clip that changes anything logs one warning (agent, tool, field, dropped), de-duplicated per (agent, tool, field, kind, dropped). A restore that still fails for another reason returns the degraded `build_failed` status (`SESSION_STATUS_BUILD_FAILED`) with a fixed sentence on the WebSocket join and `POST /api/chat`; the routine runner words it itself; connector inbound catches any build exception and logs it with the agent name.
- **Notices are derived, never stored.** Every agent response carries `notices` (`override_validation.compute_notices`): `{kind: "tool_override_narrowed", tool, field, field_title, dropped, effective, message}` for a `narrowed` or `invalid` clip, only for a tool enabled for the agent AND app-wide and only for a field the Tools tab renders (`agent_tab_renders`, mirrored by the client's `allowed()`); a clip on a hidden field or a disabled tool still applies at build and is only logged. `dropped` and `effective` keep the STORED form of path entries (not canonical). The clip is memoised (512 entries, key a sha256 of (tool, entry, merged row)); the symlink state is a third input the key cannot see, so a cached notice can stay stale until either input changes. No dismiss route exists; a notice clears when the operator fixes either side or turns the tool off for the agent.
- **Where notices show (leg 5).** The Tools tab's `App.ui.tabs` indicator ("Tools, 2 notices" through `setIndicator`), a `toolRow` flag chip per tool (`tool-override-notice-flag`, `toolRow.setFlag`), a per-field line in an opened panel, the Agents list row (an aria-hidden "Needs attention" chip in `badges[]` plus a hidden ", needs attention" in `nameSuffix`; the row signature includes the notice count) and the chat drawer's read-only "Needs attention" group (`chat-drawer-notices`, built by `drawerParts.noticesGroup`, with a link to `#agents/{name}/tools`). The agent editor exposes `editorHandle.setNotices(notices)` (indicator plus tool flags only, never a form value); the studio `agents` store subscription passes the selected agent's `notices` to it, so an open, even dirty, editor follows a Settings change.
- **The agent tab stores what the operator changes.** A field is the agent's own when its key is in the stored override, inherited otherwise; the Save walk stores own fields only and omits `tool_overrides` when the result equals the stored map. A denied list stores only the entries the agent adds (the effective list is the union). A one-time startup step (`the_one/db/override_cleanup.py::cleanup_copied_overrides`, marker `tool_override_cleanup_v1` in `app_metadata`, written once the agent loop has started) removed the copies earlier saves made: a value equal to the effective app-wide one, app-wide entries in denied lists, dead keys, emptied tool entries. A copy of an app-wide value the operator had since changed no longer matches and stays as that agent's pin.
- **Redirects are checked.** `SSRFGuardedTransport` takes `host_allowlist` / `host_denylist` and runs `_host_policy_check` (the allow list with `NO_HOSTS`, then the deny list, both sides through `normalize_host`: lowercase IDNA via `httpx.URL(...).raw_host`) on EVERY request, so a redirect hop is refused like a first URL. The IP pin is a new `httpx.Request` (headers as they are, `sni_hostname` ASCII); the caller's request is never mutated, so a relative `Location` resolves against the hostname.
- **Settings tightening preview (leg 5, DD8).** `POST /api/tools/configs/{name}/impact` takes the PUT's body and runs the PUT's own computation, `api.py::_compute_tool_config_update(cls, name, data, existing) -> (new_enabled, new_config, new_secrets)` (the merge of hidden fields, validation with the hidden-value fallback, the 422, the secret split), so the preview equals what the PUT stores. It builds the proposed `ToolConfig` row with `model_copy`, computes `compute_notices` for every agent before and after (one rows read, one agents read, in a thread) and lists, per agent, the fields whose notice for that tool is new or gained a dropped value: `{"agents": [{"agent", "fields": [{"field", "field_title", "dropped"}]}]}`. A widening, a no-op and a disabled tool list nothing; unknown tool 404. The Settings editor's `save()` asks it first (a failed preview does not block; the PUT is the gate), opens `App.confirmDialog` "Saving narrows N agents" (Save anyway / Cancel, before `focusAfter.begin`) on a non-empty answer, and after a saved config or the tile switch calls `App.store.invalidate('agents')`. Known gap: re-enabling a tool through the tile switch can add notices without a warning.
- **Shell, Python and downloads ignore an agent's own paths.** They read `read_file`'s app-wide row (`builtins.py` cross-config view; `host_access` and `DownloadFileTool` use it), and on a non-bubblewrap host Shell and Python are not path-limited at all. `GET /api/config` reports `execution_sandbox` ("bubblewrap" or "passthrough", warmed in the lifespan) and the agent tab shows a note on every Allowed/Denied paths field while one of those tools is on for the agent and app-wide. `web_search` uses the app-wide `extra_allowed_hosts` for its SSRF policy, not an agent's.

### Constraint surface

Active constraint types after F5:

| Type | Config field(s) | Marker(s) | Applies to |
|------|-----------------|-----------|------------|
| Path scope | `FilesystemConfig.path_allowlist`, `path_denylist` | `NarrowPrefix` + `NarrowSuperset` | 9 filesystem tools + cwd validation in 6 execution tools (cross-Config) + `download_file` destination |
| Read-size cap | `FilesystemConfig.read_size_cap_bytes`, `allow_binary_files` | `Narrow` × 2 | `read_file`, `grep`, indirect via `edit_file` pre-read |
| Output cap (transport ceiling) | `ExecutionConfig.output_cap_bytes` | `Narrow` | 6 execution tools (combined stdout+stderr, tail-preserving). M16 F08 L01: reclassified from a model-sized LLM cap to a transport/safety ceiling — the context-budget governor now applies the model-derived LLM bound on top (tail-preserving for shell). |
| Timeout caps | `ExecutionConfig.timeout_seconds_default`, `timeout_seconds_max` | `Narrow` × 2 + `default <= max` model_validator | 6 execution tools |
| Command denylist | `ExecutionConfig.command_denylist` | `NarrowSuperset` | `shell`, `python_exec`, `shell_session_send` (per-call re-check). Substring match with trailing-space convention to avoid the `sudoku` false-positive class. |
| Sandbox network | `ExecutionConfig.sandbox_network_allowed` | `Narrow` | `shell`, `python_exec` (deny-by-default; `True` opts the built-in tool into network access inside the bwrap jail) |
| URL/host scope + SSRF | `WebConfig.*` (see below); `host_allowlist` is `NarrowAnyWhenEmpty` (sortie 07), `NO_HOSTS` refuses every URL | mixed | 3 web tools (`http_request`, `fetch_url`, `download_file`); the allow and deny lists apply to every redirect hop, not only the first URL (sortie 07 DD15) |
| Search-engine selection | `WebSearchConfig.*` (see below) | none (operator-only) | `web_search` |

**`WebConfig.redirect_cap`** is `Field`-only — NO `Annotated` marker. Tool-level only; agent override raises `ValueError`.

**`web_search` engine registry (M16 F08 L02 — DD3 refinement).** `web_search` migrated off the shared `WebConfig` onto its own `WebSearchConfig` (it never read `WebConfig`'s SSRF/transport fields — it consumes only the injected `SearchBackend`). `WebSearchConfig` carries the engine selector + per-engine connection fields, all operator-only (NO resolver marker — agents cannot pick the search engine or set keys):

| Field | Purpose |
|-------|---------|
| `search_backend` | Active engine: `duckduckgo` (keyless default) / `searxng` / `google_pse` / `brave` / `tavily`. Dropdown (`ui_hint:"dropdown"`). |
| `searxng_base_url` | SearXNG instance base URL (JSON format must be enabled on the instance). |
| `google_pse_api_key` *(secret)* | Google Programmable Search Engine API key. |
| `google_pse_cse_id` | Google PSE engine id (the `cx` param). |
| `brave_api_key` *(secret)* | Brave Search API subscription token. |
| `tavily_api_key` *(secret)* | Tavily API key. |

The factory `build_search_backend(config, *, extra_allowed_hosts)` (`_search_backends.py`) maps `search_backend` to one of five `SearchBackend`-conformant classes (`DDGSearchBackend` / `SearxngSearchBackend` / `GooglePSESearchBackend` / `BraveSearchBackend` / `TavilySearchBackend`). Each owns a **per-call** `httpx.AsyncClient(transport=SSRFGuardedTransport(extra_allowed_hosts=…))` — even DDG respects the SSRF policy (read off the resolved shared `WebConfig`). Default DDG for unset/unknown names; a **selected-but-unconfigured keyed engine still constructs and raises `SearchUnavailableError` at call time** (no silent DDG fallback — that would hide misconfig).

**Honest-empty contract (M16 F08 L02 — HIGH-3).** Backends raise typed errors (`SearchError` base → `SearchBlockedError` / `SearchUnavailableError`) on blocked/throttled/parse-failure/misconfig, and return `[]` ONLY for a genuinely-empty result. `WebSearchTool.execute` surfaces the raised error honestly as `[error] web_search ({backend}) blocked/throttled|unavailable: …` and keeps `[]` → "No results found." — so the model stops mistaking a broken search for "the web has nothing" and guessing URLs. DDG's genuine-empty is detected via its positive `no-results` HTML marker (Option B); a 200 with neither `result__a` nor `no-results` is treated as blocked. Backends NEVER log secret values.

> **Secret-at-rest (M16 F08 L03 — landed)**: the secret fields are MARKED (`json_schema_extra={"ui_hint":"password","secret":true}`) on `WebSearchConfig`; the encrypted-at-rest `config`+`secrets` split now lands them in the `tool_configs.secrets` column (schema v32) — see "Tool config secrets storage" above. The flight batch-commits at the end (no intermediate release), so plaintext keys never ship.

**Admin config UI (M16 F08 L04 — landed).** Administration → Tools → `web_search` renders the engine registry schema-driven. The operator picks an engine from the `search_backend` **dropdown** (`ui_hint:"dropdown"` on a string `Literal` — the shared field module's `renderField` (then `tools-form-helpers.js`, deleted in M31 F5 L06; now `areas/shared/tool-config-fields.js`) has a string-enum `<select>` branch alongside the integer-dropdown branch so the picker renders as a real `<select>` rather than a free-text box) and sees **only that engine's** connection fields. Per-engine visibility is driven by a generic `show_if` mechanism: each per-engine field carries `json_schema_extra={"show_if":{"search_backend":"<engine>"}}`, and `tools-editor.js` `applyShowIf(container, properties)` hides any field whose controller value ≠ the `show_if` value (`is-hidden` + `aria-hidden="true"`), re-running on the controller's `change`. **Hidden rows stay in the DOM** (so `getFormSnapshot` still submits them — an entered key survives an engine switch; the leg-3 preserve-on-masked contract handles the masked round-trip). DuckDuckGo shows no per-engine fields. The three secret keys (`google_pse_api_key` / `brave_api_key` / `tavily_api_key`) render as **masked password inputs** with an `.api-key-eye` reveal toggle (ported from the provider editor; toggles `type` password↔text + swaps `aria-label`). On reload, GET returns the leg-3 mask (`***...{tail4}`); leaving it untouched preserves the stored key. The `show_if` mechanism is generic (reads the schema, not hardcoded to `search_backend`) — any field controlled by any sibling participates. No Studio change: all `WebSearchConfig` fields are markerless `str` and so never render in agent mode (`renderField` early-returns `''` for non-boolean markerless fields in agent mode).

**SSRF "no full-disable" mission invariant**: `WebConfig` has no `disable_ssrf` / `ssrf_disabled` / `disable_ssrf_protection` field. SSRF protection is always-on; operators reaching internal hosts list them in `extra_allowed_hosts` (host-by-host opt-in), not a global kill-switch. The invariant is encoded by the field's ABSENCE — no enforcement code needed. Sweep: `grep -rn 'disable_ssrf\|ssrf_disabled' the_one/` must return zero source hits (only docs allowed).

**Two-layer SSRF enforcement**: (1) `_url_check(url, cfg)` at the tool boundary refuses non-`http(s)` schemes, enforces host allowlist/denylist. (2) `SSRFGuardedTransport` resolves DNS once and pins the connect target to the resolved IP, refusing loopback / link-local / RFC1918 / cloud-metadata / IPv6 ULA + ULL by default. **IP-literal hosts** are also gated by `_is_ssrf_blocked(host)` in the transport's IP-literal short-circuit branch (gated BEFORE `super().handle_async_request`). `extra_allowed_hosts` is the operator override for both paths. DNS-rebind protection: the resolve-then-pin strategy means the second-call rebind to a private IP never reaches the connect layer.

### Subprocess environment invariant

The hardcoded 8-key allowlist is defined as `_ENV_ALLOWLIST` in `the_one/platform/env.py`
and materialized by `_build_subprocess_env()`:

```
PATH, HOME, USER, LOGNAME, LANG, LC_ALL, TERM, TZ
```

Call sites (M14 F1+F2 update):
- `the_one/tools/shell.py` (ShellTool) — calls `_build_subprocess_env()` and passes the
  filtered env to `run_shell()` / the sandbox seam.
- `the_one/tools/python_exec.py` (PythonExecTool) — same pattern.
- `the_one/platform/pty.py::UnixPtySession` — calls `_build_subprocess_env()` in the
  parent (before `pty.fork()`) and passes the filtered env through
  `get_sandbox_backend().prepare_spawn(...)` — now wired through the sandbox seam (M14 F2),
  identical in structure to the one-shot built-in tools.

The platform layer (`run_shell`, `run_python`) receives the already-filtered env from the
tool layer — it does NOT re-build the allowlist internally.

**Fork-safety convention (PTY spawn path)** — `pty.fork()` is a raw POSIX fork. Between
`fork()` and the subsequent `execvp()` (the OS-level exec that replaces the child image),
only **async-signal-safe** operations are permitted in the child process. In practice:
no Python-level memory allocation, no logging, no lock acquisition, no `import`, and no
calls into the asyncio event loop. Python's allocator, the GIL, and any library mutexes
are in an undefined state in the child immediately after fork — calling them before exec
can deadlock or corrupt state silently. Footgun: if the parent process is **multi-threaded**
at `fork()` time (e.g. asyncio + a thread-pool executor), the child inherits only the
forking thread's stack while all other threads' locks remain held, making even basic
C-library calls unsafe before exec. The PTY spawn path is safe today because exec follows
the fork immediately; any future change that adds work between `fork()` and `exec()` in
`UnixPtySession._start()` must audit for async-signal-safety.

**NOT operator-configurable** — part of the F4 trust contract: the agent has no path to read or carry through host secrets via subprocess inheritance.

### Threat model — trip-wire, not fence

The `command_denylist` is a **trip-wire** catching obvious-stupid (agent typos, prompt-injection telegraphs in plaintext, operator-specific dangerous patterns). It is **NOT a fence** against adversarial agents — `bash -c "$(echo c3VkbyB3aG9hbWk= | base64 -d)"` is not in the denylist. The trip-wire produces a clean small-model-readable error rather than a destructive side-effect; bypasses are accepted because the operator-trust threat model assumes the agent is not actively adversarial. Operators wanting fence-grade isolation run the agent in a sandbox (container, VM, namespace).

False-positive surface is also accepted: `echo "do not run sudo"` is refused; workaround documented in `shell_session_send`'s tool description.

### PTY session lifecycle

- **Addressing** (Decision 4): per-explicit-handle. `shell_session_start` returns a `pty-{12hex}` token; the agent passes it back into `_send`/`_close`. Four discrete tools (`_start`, `_send`, `_close`, `_list`), not an action-enum.
- **Per-agent isolation**: registry keyed on `(agent_name, session_id)`. Cross-agent `_send`/`_close` returns the SAME error shape as a non-existent session ("not owned by this agent or does not exist") — agents cannot probe for other agents' session ids.
- **Idle reap**: single lazy asyncio reaper; wakes every 30s; closes sessions where `monotonic() - last_activity > pty_session_idle_ttl_seconds` (default 5min, `Narrow`). Registered with FastAPI lifespan for clean drain on shutdown.
- **Max concurrent**: `count_for_agent < max_concurrent_sessions` (default 5, `Narrow`). The L6 spec accepts a documented race on concurrent `_start` calls; the registry's internal lock has so far serialised correctly under single-process asyncio.
- **Idle-mark prompt detection**: `_read_until_idle` blocks until the master_fd has been silent for 100ms (2s hard cap). Robust against inconsistent shell prompts (zsh themes, sudo prompts).
- **Per-call denylist re-check** (Decision 11): every `_send` runs `_command_check(input, exec_cfg)` BEFORE forwarding. Agents cannot pre-stage a denylisted command behind an open session.

### Singleton aiosqlite connection model

`the_one/db/connection.py` holds a single module-level `aiosqlite.Connection` (`_connection`). The connection is lazy — created on the first `get_connection()` call — with WAL journal mode and foreign-key enforcement applied immediately after open. All repositories and schema helpers obtain the connection via `get_connection()`; there is no connection-pool layer. The lifespan handler calls `close_connection()` on shutdown, which closes and nulls the module-level reference. Tests that need a clean state re-call `init_db()` on an isolated `tmp_path` data dir.

### Knowledge storage (Flight 2, Legs 1-4)

`kb_chunks` (v40) holds one row per extracted text chunk (`item_id`, `kb_id`, `seq`, `text`, `char_start`, `char_end`; `AUTOINCREMENT` ids, never recycled; `UNIQUE(item_id, seq)`). `kb_chunks_fts` is an external-content FTS5 index over it (`content='kb_chunks', content_rowid='id', tokenize='porter unicode61'`) — external-content means the FTS shadow table stores no text of its own and must be kept in sync by hand; SQLite's FK cascade reaches `kb_chunks` on a `kb_items`/`knowledge_bases` delete but does **not** reach `kb_chunks_fts`.

- **The repository is the only writer of `kb_chunks_fts`.** `KBChunkRepository` (`the_one/db/repositories.py`) owns every FTS write — insert, delete, rebuild, integrity-check — through the module-level `_fts_delete_rows(conn, rows)` helper (the sole site issuing the `INSERT INTO kb_chunks_fts(kb_chunks_fts, rowid, text) VALUES ('delete', ?, ?)` command) and `replace_item_chunks`'s own `INSERT INTO kb_chunks_fts(rowid, text) VALUES (?, ?)`. A raw `DELETE FROM kb_items` or `DELETE FROM knowledge_bases` issued anywhere outside `KnowledgeBaseRepository.delete` / `KBItemRepository.delete` is a defect — it desyncs the index (`check_fts()` starts returning `False`) because FK cascade silently removes the `kb_chunks` rows without their matching FTS deletes.
- **FTS delete needs the original text**: external-content FTS5 deletion requires the text that was indexed, not just the rowid, so every deleting path does `SELECT id, text FROM kb_chunks WHERE ...` **before** the matching `DELETE FROM kb_chunks` — never after. `KnowledgeBaseRepository.delete` and `KBItemRepository.delete` each do this select-then-FTS-delete step themselves, inside their own existing `write_transaction`, immediately before the row delete that FK-cascades the `kb_chunks` rows away.
- **No nesting**: `KnowledgeBaseRepository.delete` / `KBItemRepository.delete` never call into `KBChunkRepository`'s methods — each of those opens its own `write_transaction`, and `write_transaction` is not re-entrant (same-task re-entry raises `RuntimeError`, per `db-writes-hold-the-write-lock` above). They call the module-level `_fts_delete_rows` helper directly instead, composing the FTS sync into their own transaction rather than nesting one guard inside another.
- **`check_fts()` must use the two-argument integrity-check form** — `INSERT INTO kb_chunks_fts(kb_chunks_fts, rank) VALUES ('integrity-check', 1)` — which, for an external-content table, verifies the index against the content table. The one-argument form (`VALUES ('integrity-check')`) checks only the index's own internal structure and reports clean on an external-content desync; three bypass scenarios were verified against it during Leg 2's design review before this was corrected.
- **`bm25(kb_chunks_fts)` and `WHERE kb_chunks_fts MATCH ?` name the UNALIASED table**, even when the `FROM` clause aliases it (`kb_chunks_fts f`) — `bm25(f)` / `f MATCH ...` raise `no such column: f`. `search_keyword`'s KB-scoping is therefore a `JOIN kb_chunks c ON c.id = f.rowid ... AND c.kb_id = ?`, not a predicate on the FTS table itself, because the FTS table carries no `kb_id` column.
- **`bm25()` is lower-is-better (negative)** — `search_keyword(kb_id, query, k) -> list[tuple[int, float]]` returns the raw score as-is, ordered ascending (best first); callers (the Leg 4 benchmark, the later fusion flight) must not assume higher-is-better.
- **Query quoting**: `search_keyword` splits its query on whitespace and quotes each term individually (`'"' + term.replace('"', '""') + '"'`), joined by spaces (FTS5 implicit AND) — this neutralises FTS5 operator characters (`"`, `*`, `(`, `-`, `OR`, `NEAR`) so a query containing them is matched as plain terms rather than raising or being misparsed as a query-language expression. An empty/whitespace-only query returns `[]` without touching the DB.
- **`ChunkRecord`** (`the_one/db/_kb_types.py`, frozen dataclass: `seq`, `text`, `char_start`, `char_end`) is the shape crossing from the chunker (Leg 3) into `KBChunkRepository.replace_item_chunks(item_id, kb_id, chunks)` — deliberately a plain dataclass, not Pydantic, since it validates nothing itself (`UNIQUE(item_id, seq)` and `NOT NULL` enforce at insert time).
- **`the-one doctor`'s `fts5` gate** (`_probe_fts5`, unconditional per DD-11 — see "Diagnostics" above) proves FTS5 support end-to-end (in-memory `CREATE VIRTUAL TABLE ... USING fts5`, insert, `MATCH`) independently of the `kb_chunks_fts` schema — it is a platform/SQLite-build capability check, not a check of this repository's sync logic (that is `tests/test_kb_chunks.py`'s job).

**The vector-store seam** (Leg 4, DD-3/DD-4/DD-10/DD-13; `usearch`-only since Leg 6, DD-9): `the_one/vectors/_store.py` holds a `runtime_checkable` Protocol (`VectorStore`: `open`/`upsert`/`delete`/`delete_kb`/`query`/`count`/`flush`/`close`, plus `backend`/`dtype`/`dim`; structural satisfaction — no inheritance) and `quantize_int8(vectors) -> np.ndarray` (`np.clip(np.rint(v * 127), -127, 127).astype(np.int8)` — the cast is load-bearing: `usearch.Index.add` silently re-scales an un-cast float array a second time instead of raising, corrupting the stored vector). Every id crossing the seam is a `kb_chunks.id` value (Leg 1's `AUTOINCREMENT`, never recycled); no chunk text or metadata ever crosses. A candidate second backend (loadable-SQLite-extension-backed, `sqlite-vec`) was benchmarked in Legs 4-5 and removed from the closure in Leg 6 after the operator's DD-9 decision — the seam (Protocol, dispatcher shape, settings shape) is kept generic for a future second backend, but only `UsearchStore` exists today.

- **`upsert` means delete-then-insert.** `usearch.Index.add` raises on a duplicate key (no native upsert).
- **Write ordering (DD-3)**: chunks + FTS commit first (Leg 2's repository), vectors land after via this seam; an item reaches `indexed` only once the vectors have **flushed** — see the flush rule below. `UsearchStore` cannot save its HNSW index on every `upsert` at scale (a save is O(N)), so `upsert` only mutates the in-memory index and marks it dirty — `flush(kb_id)` is what calls the atomic save (below) and is what "vectors landed" means. An `upsert` observed through a FRESH `UsearchStore` instance without an intervening `flush` does not see the new vectors — documented behaviour, not a bug (empirically measured per-`flush` save cost at Leg 4's test sizes, dim 512: ~3 ms at 50 vectors, ~7.5 ms at 1,000, ~6 ms at 5,000 — sidecar bytes scale linearly, ~1.17 KB/vector at f16; Leg 5's benchmark measures the corpus-ceiling number).
- **Synthetic-corpus recall is index recall, not retrieval quality**: the Flight 2 benchmark's synthetic recall@10 column (0.04–0.43 across 20k–3M) is `usearch`'s known HNSW failure mode against a pathologically clustered generator (64 tight Gaussian clusters), not a quality regression — DD-4's 0.95 recall floor applies to the real-corpus column only, where f16 measured 0.98.
- **The store acquires `the_one/db/_write_lock.py`'s write lock itself, for every one of its own writes — a caller must never wrap a store call inside its own `write_transaction`** (not re-entrant; raises `RuntimeError` on same-task re-entry, per `db-writes-hold-the-write-lock`). This is why the write ordering above is always two transactions, never one. `UsearchStore.upsert`/`.delete` enter-and-exit an empty `write_transaction` block purely to enforce this no-nesting contract (the actual mutation is in-memory only, under the store's own per-KB `asyncio.Lock`) — a deliberate departure from Leg 4's literal per-method guidance, needed to satisfy the acceptance criterion that EVERY store method (not only the SQL-writing ones) refuses nesting.
- **`UsearchStore`** (`the_one/vectors/usearch_store.py`) is a per-KB sidecar: one HNSW index file at `{data_dir}/vectors/{kb_id}.usearch` (`data_dir` resolved inside `open()`, never at construction — a `settings.data_dir` monkeypatch applied after construction but before `open()` is honoured), with a registry table `kb_vector_index(kb_id, path, count, generation, dtype, dim, updated_at)`. The registry table was store-owned (created inside `open()`) through Legs 4-5 while the backend choice was open; Leg 6 moved its DDL into the v41 migration block (DD-9 won it), and `open()` issues no DDL at all today. Per-KB state (`_KbIndex`: the loaded `Index | None`, an `asyncio.Lock`, `dirty`/`stale` flags) is get-or-created SYNCHRONOUSLY via `dict.setdefault` — no `await` between the membership check and the insert — before anything ever awaits the per-KB lock; the lock cannot guard its own creation, so the synchronous `setdefault` is what makes two concurrent first-touches of the same `kb_id` land on the same lock instead of racing to create two independent ones.
- **Staleness** (Leg 4, hardened Leg 6): on load, a registry row whose sidecar file is missing, unreadable (`_ensure_loaded` catches the base `Exception` around `Index.restore` — a corrupt sidecar raises `ValueError` on garbage/empty bytes or `RuntimeError` on a truncated file, three different types for one condition), whose restored index length disagrees with the row's `count`, or whose `dtype`/`dim` disagree with the store's configuration marks the KB stale and raises `VectorStoreStaleError` (chained `from` the original exception when applicable) on every subsequent read/write except `rebuild_from` and `delete_kb`; `rebuild_from(kb_id, ids_and_vectors)` builds a fresh index from scratch, saves it, and clears the staleness.
- **Atomic sidecar save (Leg 6)**: `flush`/`rebuild_from` route through `_save_atomically` — save to a same-directory `.usearch.tmp` path, then `os.replace(tmp, path)` (atomic on POSIX and Windows; `Index.restore` never memory-maps, so nothing keeps the destination open), only THEN upsert the registry row. A failure before the replace leaves the previous sidecar untouched, best-effort-removes the temp file, and leaves `entry.dirty` `True` so the next `flush`/`close()` retries. The sidecar-write and registry-write remain two separate steps (pre-existing caveat): a crash between them leaves the sidecar one generation ahead of the registry, which the staleness check above reports as stale — a fail-safe window, recoverable by `rebuild_from`, not a defect.
- **`delete_kb` is guard-first (Leg 6)**: the `write_transaction` that deletes the registry row runs BEFORE any in-memory or filesystem mutation — a caller who nests this call inside their own `write_transaction` gets the re-entry `RuntimeError` before anything has changed, not after the sidecar was already unlinked (the pre-Leg-6 ordering).
- **Dimension guard (squawk 0054)**: `upsert`/`query`/`rebuild_from` all raise `ValueError` naming the expected/actual dim and the `kb_id` when a vector's last dimension != `self.dim`, checked before quantisation.
- **Dispatcher** (`the_one/vectors/__init__.py`): `get_vector_store()` is **async** (a documented departure from `create_pty_session`'s sync dispatch shape, since the backend choice is a DB record) and double-checks under an `asyncio.Lock` — the `get_embedding_backend()` shape widened to async. It reads `knowledge.vector_store` (absent → `usearch`/`f16` default, DD-9), calls the named `_build_store(settings, conn)` seam (one branch today; lazy-imports the backend module; the site AC-11's tests monkeypatch to inject a recording stub), `open()`s the store, and caches it — callers never call `open()` themselves. `_reset_for_test()` clears the cache.
- **`VectorStoreSettings`** (`the_one/knowledge/settings.py`): `backend: Literal["usearch"]`, `dtype: str` (validated against `("f16", "i8")` — `f16` is the DD-9 default), `dim`, `model_id`; stored under `knowledge.vector_store` (`VECTOR_STORE_KEY`). **Store-written, not operator-configurable** — `update_knowledge_settings`'s existing generic top-level-key guard already 422s a `vector_store` key in a `PUT` body (no dedicated message was added). Unlike `load_knowledge_settings`, `load_vector_store_settings` RAISES (never falls back) on an invalid stored record — a corrupt vector-store record is the one setting that must not silently default, because the wrong backend would read the wrong on-disk data with no error. `GET /api/knowledge-settings` surfaces it read-only under `"vector_store"`, alongside the pre-existing `notes` list.
- **Delete-route wiring**: `delete_knowledge_base` and `delete_kb_item` (`the_one/interfaces/web/api.py`) call `store.delete_kb(kb_id)` / `store.delete(kb_id, ids)` AFTER the repository transaction has committed, never inside it (the store acquires the write lock itself — nesting would raise). A store failure is logged (`logger.exception`) and swallowed; the route still returns 200 — the DB row is truth, and a stale vector row for a never-recycled id is harmless.
- **No native SQLite extension is loaded anywhere in the app** (Leg 6; see `SECURITY-MODEL.md`). `usearch` is an ordinary imported Python extension module, not a SQLite extension, and never touches the app's SQLite connection. The Leg 4 `sqlite-vec` backend, which did load a native SQLite extension (`enable_load_extension`), was removed in Leg 6.
- **An inert `kb_vectors` table can remain in a dev database** that opened the removed `sqlite-vec` backend between Leg 4 and Leg 6 (its `vec0` virtual table cannot be dropped without the extension loaded — `DROP TABLE` raises `OperationalError: no such module: vec0`). No release ever shipped this table; the v41 migration deliberately never touches it, and every other statement (including migrations) works fine with the table present and the extension absent.

**The embedding seam** (Leg 3, DD-1): `the_one/embeddings/` now holds a `runtime_checkable` Protocol (`EmbeddingBackend` in `_backend.py`: `model_id`, `dim`, `embed(texts) -> EmbedResult`, structural satisfaction — no inheritance), the production backend (`LocalStaticBackend` in `local.py`, wrapping the bundled `potion-retrieval-32M` model and carrying the two DD-5 offline-load guards `resolve_model_dir()`/`force_hf_offline()` that used to live only in the doctor probe), and a process-wide singleton (`get_embedding_backend()` / `warm_up()` / `_reset_for_test()` in `__init__.py`, double-checked-locked so two concurrent first callers still load once). The test-only second implementation, `StubEmbeddingBackend` (`tests/_embedding_stub.py`, hash-seeded deterministic unit vectors), is what every seam-consumer's unit test runs against — real loads stay confined to `tests/test_doctor_embedding_model.py::TestRealLoad` (two per run) and `tests/test_embeddings.py`'s own singleton tests (which stub `LocalStaticBackend.load`, never touching model2vec for real). A global `tests/conftest.py` autouse fixture (`_stub_embedding_backend_by_default`) patches `LocalStaticBackend.load` to a stub for every OTHER test module, since `create_knowledge_base` now fires `warm_up()` as a background task on every call.

**Knowledge settings** (Leg 3, DD-6; widened to three records by Flight 4 Leg 1's DD-5): three `app_metadata` JSON records — `knowledge.embedding`, `knowledge.chunking`, and `knowledge.monitoring` (`the_one/knowledge/settings.py`'s `EMBEDDING_KEY`/`CHUNKING_KEY`/`MONITORING_KEY`) — read with defaults applied (`load_knowledge_settings`) and written as three independent transactions (`save_knowledge_settings`). `EmbeddingSettings` uses plain `str`/`int` fields with `field_validator`s (not `Literal`s — a `Literal` mismatch can't carry the DD-2 amendment message into the 422 response) rejecting anything but the one bundled `(backend, model_id, dim)` combination. `ChunkingSettings` bounds `size_chars` (200–2000) and `overlap_chars` (`<= size_chars // 2`). `MonitoringSettings` (`extra="forbid"`) bounds `watch_enabled: bool = True` and `reconcile_interval_seconds: int = Field(300, ge=10, le=86400)`. Routes: `GET`/`PUT /api/knowledge-settings` in `api.py`, merge-then-revalidate on `PUT` (the `update_knowledge_base` precedent), 422 on an unknown top-level key, an unknown nested key under `monitoring`, or a failed validator. Unlike `chunking` (future-indexing-pass-only), a `monitoring` change is applied live: a successful `PUT` awaits `_after_monitoring_settings_changed(updated.monitoring)` (the `leg-staged-hook-seam` above), which calls the installed `WatchManager`'s `apply_settings` — the route does not return until the watcher has actually reacted to the new settings.

**The chunker** (Leg 3, DD-7): `the_one/knowledge/chunking.py::chunk_text(text, *, size_chars=1000, overlap_chars=150) -> list[ChunkRecord]` is pure and deterministic, offsets into the original text (never a normalized copy — CRLF is handled via `\r?\n` regexes, not by rewriting `\r\n` to `\n`). One combined, sorted set of candidate break positions (paragraph/line/sentence) drives both window-end selection (latest boundary at or before the size cap) and the next window's overlap start (earliest boundary at or after `end - overlap_chars`) — see the module docstring's "Overlap direction note" for why the leg spec's literal "latest boundary at or before" phrasing for the overlap step does not itself bound overlap above by `overlap_chars` (verified with concrete numbers) and was corrected during implementation.

**Knowledge indexing** (Flight 3, Leg 2): `the_one/knowledge/extractors/` is the extractor seam — a `runtime_checkable` `Extractor` Protocol (`name`, `extensions: frozenset[str]`, `extract(path) -> str`, structural satisfaction, no inheritance) and an in-tree registry (`__init__.py`, the `the_one/connectors/registry.py` shape) keyed by lower-cased dotted SUFFIX rather than by name, since one extractor claims many suffixes: `register(extractor)` inserts one dict entry per suffix in `extractor.extensions`, checking every suffix for a conflicting *different* extractor name before inserting any (all-or-nothing); re-registering the same name is a no-op; `unregister(name)` scans and removes every entry that name owns. The package `__init__` registers `TextExtractor` (`extractors/text.py`) at import via a private `_register_builtins()` (also what a test calls after `clear_for_test()` to restore the built-ins). `TextExtractor` decodes `utf-8-sig` with `errors="replace"` (the `ReadFileTool` precedent) over the DD-4 plain-text/markdown/code suffix set — `.html`/`.htm`/`.log` are deliberately absent (raw tags are poor chunk text; `readability-lxml`/`markdownify` are already in the closure for a later `HtmlExtractor`). Adding a new format is one module plus one `register` call, with zero pipeline change — pinned by `tests/test_kb_extractors.py`'s M7 test, which registers `tests/_extractor_stub.py::StubExtractor` and indexes a `.stub` file end to end with `indexer.py` untouched.

`the_one/knowledge/indexer.py::index_item(item, ctx) -> ItemOutcome` runs Flight 2's write ordering for the first time, in eight stages: (1) `update_status(pending -> indexing)` — OUTSIDE the function's try block, so `KBItemTransitionError`/`KeyError` from an already-wrong status or an unknown item propagate rather than being swallowed as a per-item failure; (2) resolve the extractor, `stat`, reject over `MAX_FILE_BYTES` (10 MiB) or on an 8 KiB binary sniff, `extract` under `to_thread`, hash the bytes (`walk.content_hash`) — two full reads (extract, hash) plus one head read (sniff), by design, never folded together; (3) `chunk_text` under `to_thread`; (4) **transaction boundary**: `ids_for_item` then `replace_item_chunks` — chunks + FTS commit in ONE transaction; (5) `store.delete(kb_id, old_ids)` if any, AFTER that commit, never inside it (the store takes its own write lock — DD-13 of Flight 2); (6)-(7) embed in `EMBED_BATCH` (256)-sized batches under `to_thread`, rows below `NORM_TOLERANCE` norm (the seam's documented zero-token edge) kept as chunks but excluded from `store.upsert` and counted in `ItemOutcome.vectors`/logged via `truncated`, then `store.upsert(kb_id, ids, vectors)` per batch (skipped when nothing survives the norm filter); (8) `set_content_identity(hash, size, mtime)` from the stat/bytes taken at stage 2. **The item stays `indexing` until the flush that covers it** — `index_item` never itself flushes or flips an item to `indexed`; that is Leg 3's worker, after `store.flush(kb_id)` succeeds. Failure paths (stage 2 onward) land `failed` via a private `_Reject` exception (message stored verbatim, no traceback logged) for expected rejections (missing file, over-cap, binary, no extractor, non-`file` item type, an `ExtractionError`'s message) or the generic `Exception` handler (`f"{type(exc).__name__}: {exc}"`, truncated to 1,000 chars, logged with traceback) for anything else — `asyncio.CancelledError` and `VectorStoreStaleError` are caught and immediately re-raised (never turned into a `failed` item), named explicitly in the except-clause so a future "simplification" cannot drop the `VectorStoreStaleError` guard (DD-6: the pass-level catch is Leg 3's). Cooperative stop: `ctx.should_stop()` is consulted before every embed batch AFTER the first (never before the first — a stopped worker that already committed chunks gains nothing skipping it, and the check bounds the delay at one batch, ~75 ms measured); a `True` returns a `"stopped"` outcome with chunks committed and no further `upsert`. `ctx.embed_lock` (optional) is held around each embed-plus-upsert pair and released between batches — `asyncio.Lock`'s FIFO fairness is what lets a concurrent waiter interleave between batches with no extra yield point needed.

`FlushPolicy` (`min_chunks=2000`, `min_seconds=15.0`, `save_multiplier=10.0`, clock-injected, pure — never calls the store itself; Leg 3's worker owns the actual `flush()` call and the `awaiting_flush` list this policy gates) triggers `should_flush()` only when `chunks_since_flush >= min_chunks` **and** `elapsed >= max(min_seconds, save_multiplier * last_flush_seconds)`. Both bounds are lower bounds, so the rule *delays* flushing — the save-multiplier term is what keeps `usearch`'s O(N)-per-flush save (it resaves the WHOLE current index every time, not a delta) from dominating a build, near the operator's ~10% overhead bar, and is also what sets the crash re-work window (~15 s for small corpora, ~2 min at 1M, ~4 min at 3M — Flight 2's measured/extrapolated Windows save curve). The chunk gate never binds at scale on Windows (2,000 inserts ≈ 14 s, under the time gate); it exists only so a burst of tiny files doesn't flush per file.

**Zero-norm and `truncated`**: a chunk whose embedded vector's norm is below `NORM_TOLERANCE` (the embedding seam's own zero-token-edge tolerance, reused here rather than inventing a second threshold — any value in (1e-6, 0.9) classifies identically) is kept in `kb_chunks`/FTS but excluded from the vector `upsert` and counted in `ItemOutcome.vectors` (never `ItemOutcome.chunks`, which always equals the full chunk count). `EmbedResult.truncated` is summed across batches and logged once at INFO naming the item id and the count when non-zero — never an error.

**`rebuild_kb(kb_id, ctx, *, reason)`** is DD-6's ONLY `VectorStoreStaleError` recovery: logs a `WARNING` naming the KB and `reason`, then streams `ctx.chunk_repo.iter_for_kb(kb_id, 1_000)`, embeds each page under `to_thread` with **no** `ctx.embed_lock` (DD-5's ordering note — this is the one place embedding runs unserialised across knowledge bases, accepted because a rebuild is exceptional), filters zero-norm rows, and yields `(ids, vectors)` into `ctx.store.rebuild_from`. The generator touches only the chunk repository and the embedding backend — never calls back into the store (`count`, `flush`, another `upsert`, …), which would deadlock against the per-KB lock `rebuild_from` holds for the whole call; this no-call-back invariant is pinned in the `VectorStore` Protocol docstring and unit-tested with a store stub that raises on every method except `rebuild_from`. `rebuild_from` widened from a synchronous `Iterable` to an `AsyncIterable[tuple[Sequence[int], np.ndarray]]` this leg (a 3M-chunk KB cannot be pre-embedded into memory — 3M x 512 x 4 bytes is 6 GB) and joined the `VectorStore` Protocol itself; `UsearchStore.rebuild_from` iterates it with `async for`.

**`entry.dirty` reorder (DD-7)**: in `UsearchStore.upsert`/`.delete`, `entry.dirty = True` is now set BEFORE the `asyncio.to_thread` mutation (in `delete`, immediately after the `entry.index is None` early return) rather than after — a `to_thread` call that raises partway through a mutation must still leave the index marked dirty, since it may already be partially mutated; setting `dirty` only on success could leave a silently-mutated index with `dirty=False`, and the next `flush` would skip it.

**Lock-ordering invariant, as corrected at design review**: **manager lock -> store per-KB lock -> write lock** is the only permitted nesting order; no code may acquire the manager or per-KB lock while holding the write lock, or the manager lock while holding a per-KB lock. `UsearchStore.upsert`/`.delete` acquire **and release** the shared write lock (the empty `write_transaction` shim) *before* taking the store's per-KB lock, and touch no SQL inside it — they do NOT nest; only `flush` and `rebuild_from` genuinely nest per-KB lock -> write lock. `rebuild_from` holds the per-KB lock across the caller's generator, so `rebuild_kb`'s generator takes no manager lock (DD-6) — the one embedding-unserialised exception noted above. Stated here and in the Leg 3 manager's own docstring so Flight 4's watcher author cannot misread it.

**Two crash-window consequences recorded for Flight 5's fusion design** (neither is a defect here — fusion must tolerate both, per the `multi-await-reads-are-not-snapshots` convention above): (a) between `replace_item_chunks` and the covering flush, `search_keyword` can return chunks of an `indexing` item whose vectors have not landed; (b) the inverse — between `replace_item_chunks` and the next flush, the saved sidecar still holds vectors for the chunk ids a re-index just deleted from `kb_chunks`, so a vector query can return ids with no `kb_chunks` row (`get_many` silently returns fewer).

**A third consequence, beside the two crash windows above (Flight 5 Leg 1, DD-2, corrected at design review)**: retrieval's query embedding never takes `IndexingManager`'s embed lock — a read-only encode needs no mutual exclusion (measured bit-identical across eight concurrent callers) — but `UsearchStore.query` DOES take the store's per-KB lock, the same one `upsert`, `flush` and `rebuild_from` hold.
